with the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: I avoid that production line whenever I can, send someone else to walk it, and shut down conversations when people start talking about the rollout or the investigation.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: I can remember the meeting before the failure and the ambulance leaving afterward, but the actual few minutes where I was giving directions and trying to help are broken up and partly just gone.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: I keep thinking I can't trust my own judgment anymore, like if I miss one detail on a schedule or a machine setting, somebody else is going to get hurt because of me.\n- key: S075\n  name: Social_Detachment\n  description: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.\n  manifestation: At home I sit through family meals like I'm behind glass, hearing people talk and laugh but feeling like I don't really belong in the room with them.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I snap at operators, supervisors, and my wife over small questions, and there have been times I've slammed a clipboard or barked at someone before I even knew I was angry.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: On the floor I'm constantly checking gauges, exits, people's hands, and where the emergency stops are, and even in meetings I pick a seat where I can see the door.\n- key: S080\n  name: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: If a forklift backs up, a door bangs, or a plant alarm chirps, my whole body jumps like something is exploding, and it can take me several minutes before my hands stop shaking.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I've had to hand off floor responsibilities I used to manage myself, and I lost my composure during a regional director interview badly enough that I walked out. I mean, I don't really feel sad or... or down, exactly. It's more like what I was just saying. I just don't feel much of anything at all. It's just kind of blank.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a major stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it did. Um... this has all been going on since the retrofit accident. That was about a year and seven months ago now.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had the feeling that you yourself aren’t real, or that the world around you feels unreal?\n\nPatient: No, um... I don't think so. I know I'm real, and the world around me is real. It's not like I'm walking around in a dream or anything like that. It's just... like I said, I just feel kind of blank or disconnected from it all. But I haven't really felt like things are actually unreal, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: Like I said before, I wouldn't really call it sad. I don't wake up feeling down or like crying or anything. It's really just that blankness. I just feel kind of empty, not sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. That's actually a big part of why I'm here. I just... I don't feel any real happiness anymore. Even with things I used to like. Like, my wife tries to get me to enjoy our old Sunday routines, or even if something goes really well at work... I just don't feel anything good. No happiness, no real closeness to anyone. It's just flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you have unwanted memories of the accident that keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It doesn't take much to trigger them, either. Just... normal things at work. A standard plant alarm going off, looking over a retrofit checklist, or sometimes even just the smell of hot metal. It just... it throws me right back there. I'm right back to seeing him on the floor, hearing everyone yelling for help. It just keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I avoid that specific production line whenever I can. If it needs to be walked, I'll just send someone else to do it. And... if people start talking about the rollout, or the investigation into what happened, I just shut the conversation down. I just don't want to deal with it.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been feeling constantly on guard or watchful, like you can’t relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I just can't seem to turn it off. When I'm out on the floor, I'm constantly checking the gauges, looking at the exits, watching people's hands... making sure I know exactly where the emergency stops are at all times. And it's not just out there, either. Even if I'm just sitting in a conference room for a meeting, I always make sure to pick a seat where I can see the door. I just can't let my guard down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It just keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I avoid that specific production line whenever I can. I just can't seem to turn it off. I just can't let my guard down.\n\nDoctor: During the accident, did you believe that you or someone else might die or be seriously injured?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. It was just chaos, and I was mostly just reacting to the shouting. I wasn't really thinking ahead to how bad it might be.\n\nDoctor: Did you see someone get seriously injured in the accident?\n\nPatient: No, not the actual moment it happened. I just... I heard all the yelling first. By the time I turned around to look at the line, he was already down on the floor. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Nice to meet you too. I guess what brings me in is... well, I just don't feel real happiness or closeness anymore. It's hard to explain, but even when my wife tries to get me to enjoy our old Sunday routines, or if something goes really well at work, I just don't feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I would call it depressed. I mean, I don't really feel sad or... or down, exactly. It's more like what I was just saying. I just don't feel much of anything at all. It's just kind of blank.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a major stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it did. Um... this has all been going on since the retrofit accident. That was about a year and seven months ago now.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had the feeling that you yourself aren’t real, or that the world around you feels unreal?\n\nPatient: No, um... I don't think so. I know I'm real, and the world around me is real. It's not like I'm walking around in a dream or anything like that. It's just... like I said, I just feel kind of blank or disconnected from it all. But I haven't really felt like things are actually unreal, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: Like I said before, I wouldn't really call it sad. I don't wake up feeling down or like crying or anything. It's really just that blankness. I just feel kind of empty, not sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. That's actually a big part of why I'm here. I just... I don't feel any real happiness anymore. Even with things I used to like. Like, my wife tries to get me to enjoy our old Sunday routines, or even if something goes really well at work... I just don't feel anything good. No happiness, no real closeness to anyone. It's just flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you have unwanted memories of the accident that keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It doesn't take much to trigger them, either. Just... normal things at work. A standard plant alarm going off, looking over a retrofit checklist, or sometimes even just the smell of hot metal. It just... it throws me right back there. I'm right back to seeing him on the floor, hearing everyone yelling for help. It just keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I avoid that specific production line whenever I can. If it needs to be walked, I'll just send someone else to do it. And... if people start talking about the rollout, or the investigation into what happened, I just shut the conversation down. I just don't want to deal with it.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been feeling constantly on guard or watchful, like you can’t relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I just can't seem to turn it off. When I'm out on the floor, I'm constantly checking the gauges, looking at the exits, watching people's hands... making sure I know exactly where the emergency stops are at all times. And it's not just out there, either. Even if I'm just sitting in a conference room for a meeting, I always make sure to pick a seat where I can see the door. I just can't let my guard down.\n\nDoctor: During the accident, did you believe that you or someone else might die or be seriously injured?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. It all happened so fast that I don't think I had time to process it like that. I mean, I knew it was bad when I saw him on the floor, but... my brain wasn't really forming thoughts about whether he was going to die or not right in that exact moment. It was just chaos, and I was mostly just reacting to the shouting. I wasn't really thinking ahead to how bad it might be.\n\nDoctor: Did you see someone get seriously injured in the accident?\n\nPatient: No, not the actual moment it happened. I just... I heard all the yelling first. By the time I turned around to look at the line, he was already down on the floor. trying to help are broken up and partly just gone.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: I keep thinking I can't trust my own judgment anymore, like if I miss one detail on a schedule or a machine setting, somebody else is going to get hurt because of me.\n- key: S075\n  name: Social_Detachment\n  description: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.\n  manifestation: At home I sit through family meals like I'm behind glass, hearing people talk and laugh but feeling like I don't really belong in the room with them.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I snap at operators, supervisors, and my wife over small questions, and there have been times I've slammed a clipboard or barked at someone before I even knew I was angry.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: On the Sleep has been... really difficult. Most nights, I just lie there in the dark, replaying the plant in my head, over and over. And then, when I finally do manage to fall asleep, I either jolt awake from dreams about the assembly line, or I just wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning. And even when I wake up, I feel like I never really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Did the coworker you found on the floor have injuries that looked life-threatening or very severe?\n\nPatient: No, not really. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1, F43.2, F32.1, F33.1, F41.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Nice to meet you too. I guess what brings me in is... well, I just don't feel real happiness or closeness anymore. It's hard to explain, but even when my wife tries to get me to enjoy our old Sunday routines, or if something goes really well at work, I just don't feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I would call it depressed. I mean, I don't really feel sad or... or down, exactly. It's more like what I was just saying. I just don't feel much of anything at all. It's just kind of blank.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a major stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it did. Um... this has all been going on since the retrofit accident. That was about a year and seven months ago now.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had the feeling that you yourself aren’t real, or that the world around you feels unreal?\n\nPatient: No, um... I don't think so. I know I'm real, and the world around me is real. It's not like I'm walking around in a dream or anything like that. It's just... like I said, I just feel kind of blank or disconnected from it all. But I haven't really felt like things are actually unreal, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: Like I said before, I wouldn't really call it sad. I don't wake up feeling down or like crying or anything. It's really just that blankness. I just feel kind of empty, not sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. That's actually a big part of why I'm here. I just... I don't feel any real happiness anymore. Even with things I used to like. Like, my wife tries to get me to enjoy our old Sunday routines, or even if something goes really well at work... I just don't feel anything good. No happiness, no real closeness to anyone. It's just flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you have unwanted memories of the accident that keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It doesn't take much to trigger them, either. Just... normal things at work. A standard plant alarm going off, looking over a retrofit checklist, or sometimes even just the smell of hot metal. It just... it throws me right back there. I'm right back to seeing him on the floor, hearing everyone yelling for help. It just keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I avoid that specific production line whenever I can. If it needs to be walked, I'll just send someone else to do it. And... if people start talking about the rollout, or the investigation into what happened, I just shut the conversation down. I just don't want to deal with it.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been feeling constantly on guard or watchful, like you can’t relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I just can't seem to turn it off. When I'm out on the floor, I'm constantly checking the gauges, looking at the exits, watching people's hands... making sure I know exactly where the emergency stops are at all times. And it's not just out there, either. Even if I'm just sitting in a conference room for a meeting, I always make sure to pick a seat where I can see the door. I just can't let my guard down.\n\nDoctor: During the accident, did you believe that you or someone else might die or be seriously injured?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. It all happened so fast that I don't think I had time to process it like that. I mean, I knew it was bad when I saw him on the floor, but... my brain wasn't really forming thoughts about whether he was going to die or not right in that exact moment. It was just chaos, and I was mostly just reacting to the shouting. I wasn't really thinking ahead to how bad it might be.\n\nDoctor: Did you see someone get seriously injured in the accident?\n\nPatient: No, not the actual moment it happened. I just... I heard all the yelling first. By the time I turned around to look at the line, he was already down on the floor. So I didn't see the machinery actually hit him or anything like that, just... just what it looked like right after.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you found yourself blaming yourself for what happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... I just keep thinking that I can't trust my own judgment anymore. As the manager, I'm supposed to be on top of things, and now I just feel like, if I miss even one small detail on a schedule, or maybe overlook a machine setting... somebody else is going to get hurt because of me. So, yeah, I guess I do put a lot of that on myself.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had nightmares about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Sleep has been... really difficult. Most nights, I just lie there in the dark, replaying the plant in my head, over and over. And then, when I finally do manage to fall asleep, I either jolt awake from dreams about the assembly line, or I just wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning. And even when I wake up, I feel like I never really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Did the coworker you found on the floor have injuries that looked life-threatening or very severe?\n\nPatient: No, not really. So, yeah, I definitely feel like it's my fault.\n\nDoctor: After the accident, did you learn that your coworker had been seriously injured?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't. Um, like I said, it didn't look too bad at the time, and... well, I never heard anything different after the fact. I mean, I don't really feel sad or... or down, exactly. It's more like what I was just saying. I just don't feel much of anything at all. It's just kind of blank.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a major stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it did. Um... this has all been going on since the retrofit accident. That was about a year and seven months ago now.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had the feeling that you yourself aren’t real, or that the world around you feels unreal?\n\nPatient: No, um... I don't think so. I know I'm real, and the world around me is real. It's not like I'm walking around in a dream or anything like that. It's just... like I said, I just feel kind of blank or disconnected from it all. But I haven't really felt like things are actually unreal, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: Like I said before, I wouldn't really call it sad. I don't wake up feeling down or like crying or anything. It's really just that blankness. I just feel kind of empty, not sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. That's actually a big part of why I'm here. I just... I don't feel any real happiness anymore. Even with things I used to like. Like, my wife tries to get me to enjoy our old Sunday routines, or even if something goes really well at work... I just don't feel anything good. No happiness, no real closeness to anyone. It's just flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you have unwanted memories of the accident that keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It doesn't take much to trigger them, either. Just... normal things at work. A standard plant alarm going off, looking over a retrofit checklist, or sometimes even just the smell of hot metal. It just... it throws me right back there. I'm right back to seeing him on the floor, hearing everyone yelling for help. It just keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I avoid that specific production line whenever I can. If it needs to be walked, I'll just send someone else to do it. And... if people start talking about the rollout, or the investigation into what happened, I just shut the conversation down. I just don't want to deal with it.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been feeling constantly on guard or watchful, like you can’t relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I just can't seem to turn it off. When I'm out on the floor, I'm constantly checking the gauges, looking at the exits, watching people's hands... making sure I know exactly where the emergency stops are at all times. And it's not just out there, either. Even if I'm just sitting in a conference room for a meeting, I always make sure to pick a seat where I can see the door. I just can't let my guard down.\n\nDoctor: During the accident, did you believe that you or someone else might die or be seriously injured?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. It all happened so fast that I don't think I had time to process it like that. I mean, I knew it was bad when I saw him on the floor, but... my brain wasn't really forming thoughts about whether he was going to die or not right in that exact moment. It was just chaos, and I was mostly just reacting to the shouting. I wasn't really thinking ahead to how bad it might be.\n\nDoctor: Did you see someone get seriously injured in the accident?\n\nPatient: No, not the actual moment it happened. I just... I heard all the yelling first. By the time I turned around to look at the line, he was already down on the floor. It's... I just keep thinking that I can't trust my own judgment anymore. So, yeah, I guess I do put a lot of that on myself.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you had nightmares about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Sleep has been... really difficult. Most nights, I just lie there in the dark, replaying the plant in my head, over and over. And even when I wake up, I feel like I never really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Did the coworker you found on the floor have injuries that looked life-threatening or very severe?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, he was definitely hurt, but... it didn't look like he was going to die or anything. It was bad, but not life-threatening.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the accident happened because of something you did or failed to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It doesn't take much to trigger them, either. Just... normal things at work. A standard plant alarm going off, looking over a retrofit checklist, or sometimes even just the smell of hot metal. It just... it throws me right back there. I'm right back to seeing him on the floor, hearing everyone yelling for help. It just keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I avoid that specific production line whenever I can. If it needs to be walked, I'll just send someone else to do it. And... if people start talking about the rollout, or the investigation into what happened, I just shut the conversation down. I just don't want to deal with it.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you been feeling constantly on guard or watchful, like you can’t relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I just can't seem to turn it off. I just can't let my guard down.\n\nDoctor: During the accident, did you believe that you or someone else might die or be seriously injured?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. It all happened so fast that I don't think I had time to process it like that. I mean, I knew it was bad when I saw him on the floor, but... my brain wasn't really forming thoughts about whether he was going to die or not right in that exact moment. It was just chaos, and I was mostly just reacting to the shouting. I wasn't really thinking ahead to how bad it might be.\n\nDoctor: Did you see someone get seriously injured in the accident?\n\nPatient: No, not the actual moment it happened. I just... I heard all the yelling first. By the time I turned around to look at the line, he was already down on the floor. Like I said before, I just keep thinking that I can't trust my own judgment anymore. I mean, as the manager, I'm supposed to be the one making sure everything runs right. I mean, from what I was told, it wasn't anything major.\n\nDoctor: Before the accident, had you ever gone through a period like this where, for at least a couple of weeks, you lost interest or pleasure in things most days?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Before the accident, things were... fine, really. I enjoyed my normal routine, liked my job, and had things I liked to do on the weekends.
